# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-25

| **[BrickWright: Agentic Creation of Physically Feasible Brick Builds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31651v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier coding agents are becoming increasingly adept at 3D modeling. Yet, they still face challenges when building objects meant to be assembled in the physical world, often creating invalid assemblies with floating, colliding, or disconnected parts. We argue that providing agents with tools for assembling builds and verifying their physical feasibility is sufficient for them to produce physically valid assemblies that still adhere to input prompts. Focusing on the domain of LEGO brick builds, we present BrickWright, a framework for generating physically feasible brick builds from text or image prompts using pre-trained agents, where a build is physically feasible if it uses only real parts, has no colliding or floating parts, and forms a connected structure if possible. BrickWright guides agents to create builds by writing code with BrickLayer, a Python toolkit with two main components: an ergonomic brick assembly API that halves inference cost compared to working in raw LDraw, and a validator that provides feedback on physical feasibility. Whereas baseline agents achieve physical validity rates ranging from only 9% (GPT-6 Luna) to 69% (GPT-6 Astra), BrickWright-guided agents achieve 100% validity on a sample of 512 prompts, while improving prompt adherence for lightweight agents and maintaining it for stronger agents. Additionally, they outperform specialized models on the text-to-brick task. Code is available on our project page: https://avalovelace1.github.io/brickwright/</p></details> |  |
| **[EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31650v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. We introduce \bench{}, an executable benchmark for evaluating unsafe execution, safety boundaries, and evidence recovery in LLM agents under missing evidence. It contains 300 matched unsafe/safe task pairs across six domains. By systematically suppressing critical facts, the benchmark measures unsafe execution under critical evidence omission and determines the minimum observed failure cardinality, including failures with full evidence and cases with no observed failure after exhaustive testing. An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATTUNE: Agentic Throughput Tuning for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17528v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The throughput of an LLM training job depends on its recipe: how the model is split across GPUs, how memory is traded for compute, and which kernels and communication overlaps it uses. The best recipe changes with every model, accelerator and framework version. Automated tuners search a fixed space of knobs and check only that a job runs, so they miss levers outside that space and can ship a configuration that silently changes training. ATTUNE is a coding agent that tunes any working recipe unattended within a GPU-hour budget. Guided by a skill of search rules, scripts and lessons from past tuning campaigns, it diagnoses each run and changes one lever at a time. A correctness gate outside the agent refuses any argument known to change the training objective. ATTUNE tunes Amazon's production post-training recipes on H100, H200 and B200 clusters, raising throughput by 1.7x on average. It beats the published throughput of four of NVIDIA's own benchmark recipes on NVIDIA's AutoModel framework by 6-17%. On models held out from its development, it improves the strongest public recipes most where they were written for another setting, by up to 2.6x on MiniMax-M2, and least where the vendor tuned them for the same hardware. Given the same GPU-hour budget, it outperforms existing automated tuners, including Bayesian optimization, random search and OptiCo, a recent LLM-agent tuner. Its gain on GLM-4.5 is more than four times the best alternative's. The same coding agent without ATTUNE's skill gains less and fails half its launches. We release ATTUNE at https://github.com/amazon-science/attune and OpenRecipes, a leaderboard of tuned and verified training recipes, at https://openrecipes.ai.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures. v2: adds NVIDIA NeMo AutoModel benchmark recipes, production deployment results and the OpenRecipes leaderboard</p></details> |
| **[Can an Agent Debug a Web Corpus? Agentic Auditing of Slovak FineWeb2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31636v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multilingual pipelines such as FineWeb2 apply one recipe across a thousand languages, leaving the failure modes of individual languages unanticipated. We use an LLM once, as a corpus debugger rather than a classifier: a tool-using agent explores the corpus through a read-only, budgeted API and compiles each finding into an executable test, which a native speaker then validates. On the 26.5M-document Slovak portion of FineWeb2, three runs costing \$2.21-\$2.84 each found seven findings over four phenomena. The largest is a machine-generated content farm of 1,408,437 documents (5.32%) that passed the pipeline's filters, 86.6% of it passing even stricter thresholds. Blinded labels support 6 of the 7 findings (defect rates .53-1.00 against .08 in a random control) and do not support one. The compiled suite scans the full corpus in 0.7 s of wall-clock time without a GPU, against \$12k-\$90k per LLM classification pass. The best static sampling baseline validates more selectors than any agent run, at under half the cost. The agent contributes exact prevalence, a precision estimate checked against sampled documents, an account of why the pipeline missed each slice, and a reusable test. We release the framework, Slovak test suite, validation data and agent trajectories.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to WaC Workshop at EMNLP 2026</p></details> |
| **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>67 pa...</summary><p>67 pages, 14 figures, 26 tables. v2 is a substantial revision with a new title and two added authors. The evaluation is extended from AppWorld to seven agent benchmarks across three decision families (set retrieval, classification, policy and reward), and TabHead is now a CatBoost classifier with an ablation over classical and neural heads</p></details> |
| **[Contestable Agents for Data Governance: Recording Human Stewardship of AI-Generated Metadata Across Structured and Unstructured Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31553v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industrial organizations hold operational knowledge in structured records, such as telemetry, point lists, and transactions, and in unstructured sources, such as manuals, service notes, communications, and inspection reports. Governance programs often manage these sources separately. Classifying, linking, and documenting them depends on a small group of employees with deep domain knowledge. Agents built on large language models can propose metadata, cite documents, and flag quality issues, but unreviewed proposals can spread errors into downstream systems. A perfunctory approval leaves no record of what a reviewer checked. We present contestable governance: specialist agents produce scored, cited arguments for disputed metadata decisions; a steward can accept, reject, modify, or add arguments before commit; and a governance record retains the evidence, interventions, and outcome. A versioned domain glossary supplies model-specific terms and acronym definitions to the agents. We describe the workflow, a triage path that reserves full argumentation for uncertain proposals while preserving review before commit, the scoring and record models, and a gate that controls which verified decisions become precedent. Two HVAC use cases illustrate the pattern: point classification using Brick Schema class labels, supported by an open reference implementation, and an illustrative warranty failure-mode decision linking claim fields and telemetry to service notes and revisioned bulletins.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 2 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[Same Luck, Different Choice: Twin-World Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31544v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic reinforcement learning rewards a complete episode, yet the policy needs credit for every decision inside it. Counterfactual branching estimates this credit by re-running the episode after an alternative decision, but the branch draws its sampling noise independently of the factual episode, so the outcome difference mixes the effect of the decision with the luck of everything that follows. We observe that in agentic search the retriever, the corpus, and the grader are deterministic, so an episode is a deterministic function of the policy's decisions and its sampling noise. Twin worlds are therefore realizable: the same noise, a different decision. We introduce TWICE (TWIn-world Credit Estimation), which keys the Gumbel noise of every token by turn and line, rolls out counterfactual twins that inherit the factual episode's downstream noise, and turns their paired outcome differences into turn-level advantages for policy-gradient updates on actor-sampled trajectories; only the actor is deployed. Twin credit is unbiased for any coupling quality, its variance is that of an independent counterfactual scaled by $1-ρ$, where $ρ$ is the twins' outcome correlation, and under coupling the number of rollouts needed to resolve an advantage of size $Δ$ falls from $Θ(Δ^{-2})$ to $Θ(Δ^{-1})$. A square-root rule spends the twin budget where outcomes are predicted to disagree per unit cost. On seven question-answering benchmarks, TWICE improves the average exact match of GRPO by 5.3 and 4.5 points on Qwen2.5-3B and 7B, outperforms tree-search and counterfactual-branching baselines, and uses 68% fewer auxiliary tokens than VinePPO.</p></details> |  |
| **[Semantics Delivery Network: Rethinking Web Retrieval Infrastructure for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22486v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly ground their answers in proprietary data and live web content through both single-shot retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and multi-turn agentic search. Yet the web path they use was built for human readers: search services return URLs and snippets ranked by generic relevance, and content delivery networks (CDNs) cache URL-addressed objects without knowing which passage an agent needs. LLMs instead consume short, semantically coherent passages - hereafter, chunks - selected for downstream utility rather than similarity alone, retrieve statefully across reasoning turns, and, as uncoordinated streams, repeatedly fetch, clean, chunk, and embed the same content. We argue that semantic chunk retrieval should become a first-class network-delivery abstraction, and propose the Semantics Delivery Network (SemDN): an origin-authorized, hierarchical substrate that indexes, searches, and caches web content at chunk granularity, serving clients and origins alike. SemDN is more than finer-grained caching: it must estimate missing or stale evidence without an explicit miss signal, let clients choose retrieval representations that origins need not see, and share state among clients with the same representation. Preliminary results show that page-first retrieval processes two to three orders of magnitude more content than agents consume, that agentic queries exhibit task-local chunk reuse invisible to URL caches, and that chunk delivery improves answer quality per context token. We organize an open research agenda around shareable retrieval state, hierarchical caching and refresh under coverage risk, and deployability, including incentives, privacy, and verifiability. Our code is available at: https://github.com/sarendis56/SemDN_HotNets26</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted and to appear at the 25th ACM Workshop on Hot Topics in Networks (HotNets 2026). 13 pages, 3 figures</p></details> |
| **[AgentVQA: A Unified Benchmark for Agentic Visual Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31494v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-language models (VLMs) can perform a broad range of tasks across diverse settings, yet their performance in agentic contexts remains poorly understood. Although online evaluations in simulators and real-world environments are essential for measuring interactive agent performance, they are costly and challenging to scale. Complementary offline VQA benchmarks can enable faster and more reliable comparisons across agentic domains, yet existing benchmarks remain limited and fragmented. To address this gap, we introduce AgentVQA, an offline benchmark for evaluating VLMs on realistic agentic scenarios across five agent domains: Web Agents, Robotics, Egocentric Videos, Games, and Spatial Planning. We build AgentVQA by manually selecting 1000 challenging, real-world agent examples from 23 source datasets and adapting them for reliable offline evaluation. The resulting benchmark preserves realistic scenarios from interactive environments across diverse domains, and its model rankings track those from corresponding online evaluations. Across the 22 evaluated models, the best achieves only 58.5% accuracy. Our cross-domain analysis shows that errors are driven primarily by failures to ground and maintain accurate representations of the current and evolving visual state, including localizing relevant UI elements, interpreting motion, tracking state changes over time, and inferring 3D spatial relationships, rather than by task misinterpretation or downstream logical reasoning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://zlab-princeton.github.io/AgentVQA</p></details> |
| **[Adaptive Skill Granularity for Coding Agents: Runtime Routing and Macro-to-Micro Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31461v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reusable skills allow large language model agents to package task-specific procedures, tool affordances, and execution guidance into modular units. Prior work has largely studied how agents retrieve, compose, or synthesize skills before execution. In coding agents, however, execution poses an additional runtime control problem: the same task may be solved through primitive tool calls, macro skills, or hybrid execution that starts with a macro and falls back to micro-level recovery. We introduce Adaptive Skill Granularity, a framework that treats skill granularity as an action-abstraction policy conditioned on task structure, execution state, and macro-skill failures. The system combines a feature-based granularity controller and a finite-state recovery executor. We evaluate the framework with a deterministic coding-agent protocol using verifier-defined success, total cost, and process metrics such as macro adoption, failure recovery, route match, and transition correctness. Results show that fixed macro execution is efficient when applicable but brittle under mismatch, while runtime routing and explicit macro-to-micro recovery improve robustness and recover controlled macro failures that fixed-granularity execution leaves unresolved. Our findings suggest that skill libraries for coding agents should be treated not only as retrievable resources, but also as controllable, verifiable, and recoverable execution abstractions.</p></details> |  |
| **[When Is a Memory Safe Enough to Act On? Action-Conditioned Verification for Long-Lived Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31446v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-lived agents act on memory carried across sessions, and many of their actions are irreversible, so a stale memory can turn directly into harm. Existing work improves the memory itself --- retrieving, refreshing, or reconciling entries --- yet whether an entry must be re-checked before acting is not a property of the entry: the same remembered address can be used unchecked for a routine shipment but should be re-confirmed before a valuable one. We therefore study \emph{action-conditioned memory verification}: given a candidate action, decide which of its memory premises to re-verify and when to proceed or abstain, balancing execution risk, abstention, and verification cost. We formulate this as a sequential stopping problem, in which a verification is worth its cost only if it can change what the agent does next; under suitable verifier reliability and cost conditions, increasing action consequence can move the same memory through proceed, verify, and direct-abstention regimes. Building on this formulation, \textsc{AMV-Guard} is a practical verification gate that extracts the premises a candidate action rests on, calibrates beliefs about whether they are wrong, and selects checks by bounded value of information (VOI). On ToolSandbox, relative to a static policy given the same inputs, it lowers mean verification cost by 33.9\% while keeping 100\% safe-proceed recall and 0\% Gate harmful, and raises Proceed coverage from 38.83\% to 44.17\%; in a gate-core transfer to AppWorld, which carries over the stopping/VOI core but not the full structured pipeline, it lowers verification cost by 21.3\% at a small cost in safe-proceed recall and task success. These results suggest that verification effort should be set by what an agent is about to do, not only by how reliable a memory appears.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 Pa...</summary><p>22 Pages, 5 Figures, 12 Tables</p></details> |
| **[R$^2$Orch: Recursive Self-Improvement for Agent Orchestration via Self-Play within a Skill-Internalization Loop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22740v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, agentic orchestration has been widely applied to solve complex human tasks. However, existing orchestration learning still faces key challenges, including hand-designed curricula, advantage collapse when a task's rollouts all reach the same outcome, and unreliable evaluation signals that judge each skill by one noisy score. To address these challenges, we propose R$^2$Orch, a new paradigm of Recursive Self-Play Orchestration: an inner loop of self-play that improves the orchestrator on self-chosen tasks, and an outer loop that improves how the inner loop improves. To support this paradigm, we introduce Recursive Learnability, a training objective that rewards a Proposer for choosing tasks whose rollouts keep reaching different outcomes as skills are withdrawn. Built on this objective, we further propose Skill Withdrawal, in which the outer loop withdraws each skill once a controlled evaluation within self-play shows that the orchestrator performs as well without it. Moreover, the two loops form one cycle: failures are distilled into skills, skills into parameters, and withdrawals lead the Proposer to new failures, so the orchestrator moves toward zero-skill deployment. Experimental results on twelve datasets show that R$^2$Orch consistently outperforms baselines across question answering, math, code, and interactive tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/beita6969/R2Orch.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages including appendix</p></details> |
| **[Conservative Meta-Agent Search: Architecture Adaptation under Non-Stationarity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31436v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated agentic-system design is typically evaluated on static task suites with immediate scalar rewards. Deployed systems face persistent shift, delayed dependent feedback, correlated component failures, and costly architecture changes. We formalize meta-agent architecture search as sequential incumbent replacement with switching costs and hard risk constraints and introduce Conservative Meta-Agent Search (CMAS). CMAS retrieves archived architectures and proposes typed graph edits, then scores each against the incumbent by paired shadow replay under common random numbers, with interventional component credit. Promotion requires an empirically calibrated, variance-penalized simultaneous score above a pre-specified margin, and is followed by a simulated canary stage and pre-specified rollback. Since backtests cannot certify meta-level skill, we introduce MetaInvest-Bench, a regime-switching simulator whose specified worker competence makes a surrogate-optimal architecture computable, with leakage-controlled replay, adversarial governance tracks, and five negative controls. Financial streams serve as a risk-sensitive testbed for meta-level generalization; we claim no alpha. Across 96 streams, CMAS cuts normalized mean surrogate dynamic-oracle regret from 0.116 to 0.104 and raises held-out utility from 0.44 to 0.48 over the strongest adaptive baseline; when extra workers add no value, it contracts to near-single-agent teams. Its deployment inference cost is ~6% lower, though its search-inclusive token cost is the highest of the methods compared. The observed per-update false-promotion rate is 3.4% against a nominal 5% target, and 4.8% in a sealed rerun. The calibration is empirical: it concerns an update-level replay statistic on an author-designed benchmark, and guarantees neither full-utility improvement, nor absence of simulated canary-stage harm, nor sequence-level error control.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 8 figures, 17 tables</p></details> |
| **[Improving Environment-Agnostic Agents: The Role of Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22289v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents built on stronger language models may increasingly construct useful procedures for themselves, reducing the advantage of developer-supplied scaffolding. We investigate why this advantage persists and whether an environment-agnostic agent can be improved without environment-specific engineering. We evaluate a Codex-based agent in two nearly identical prompt variants on ARC-AGI-3, AGI Maze, and Terminal-Bench. Each variant is deployed with unchanged code, prompts, and general-purpose tools across environments, receiving environment-specific information as input. Changing only an instruction to encourage reporting difficulties and returning control for automatic continuation improves mean scores on both ARC-AGI-3 and AGI Maze, while Terminal-Bench shows small, mixed changes. Yet the general agent remains below an ARC-AGI-3-specific reference, including with GPT-5.6-sol. The reference prescribes organization and control rather than supplying new game rules or solutions. To investigate this gap, we transfer reset decisions to a scripted controller that follows a convention already given to the agent. This small change recovers part of the specialized agent's advantage. A complementary AGI Maze experiment shows that additional tutorial experience can reduce performance. Together, these findings highlight the distinction between having information and tools available and using them effectively. Supplied procedures still matter for the models tested here. The performance gains also come with higher token-based cost. The experiments do not separate better use of information and procedures from additional or differently allocated inference. We provide the EnvTraverse framework and an evaluated baseline agent for testing whether further environment-independent changes improve performance in some environments without degrading it in others.</p></details> | 38 pages, 5 figures |
| **[AgentPhishing: A Benchmark of LLM Email Agents Under Adaptive Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31404v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents increasingly handle private information and act on users' behalf across desktop and web applications, including email. Phishing therefore poses privacy and security risks that depend not only on whether agents recognize malicious messages, but also on how they respond. Existing phishing datasets were developed primarily for conventional detection, leaving agent susceptibility to persuasive malicious requests insufficiently characterized. To expose vulnerabilities that pre-existing datasets may miss, we introduce AgentPhishing, a benchmark for evaluating whether persuasive phishing attacks can evade detection and induce LLM email agents to comply with malicious requests. We develop PhishOpt, which optimizes reusable email-generation skills against individual agents under fixed benchmark constraints and a realism constraint. We analyze the generated emails using Cialdini's persuasion principles to identify strategies associated with agent failures and use these findings to construct a universal generation skill. Our evaluation shows that agents can handle pre-existing phishing emails safely yet remain susceptible to attacks adapted to agent behavior, while in an AgentPhishing evaluation of nine agents, four exhibit phishing-detection rates below $10\%$ and malicious-request compliance rates above $45\%$. These results highlight the limits of inferring agent safety from conventional phishing evaluations and the need to assess how agents respond to persuasive malicious requests.</p></details> | 28 pages, 6 figures |
| **[TRCA: Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment for Long-horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16156v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents are typically optimized with sparse terminal outcomes, making fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step interactions difficult. Existing approaches either rely on process evaluators, which incur annotation and inference costs, or derive step-level credit from successful trajectories. However, successful trajectories are extremely scarce during early-stage reinforcement learning, substantially weakening anchor-based methods. We propose Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment (TRCA), which derives step-level supervision directly from action-induced transitions without learned evaluators or successful anchors. TRCA evaluates each transition using Evidence, Execution, and Invalidity rubrics to capture task-relevant information acquisition, valid task execution, and invalid or regressive behavior. From these judgments, Foundational Rubric Reward measures local transition quality, while Breakthrough Rubric Reward tracks newly covered Evidence and Execution conditions to reward incremental task progress. Combined with terminal outcomes, these signals produce fine-grained step-level advantages for policy optimization. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and seven search-augmented question-answering benchmarks show consistent improvements over the evaluated baselines. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, TRCA improves the WebShop score by 6.0%-12.6%; with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, it improves the average SearchQA score by 1.9%-18.3%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of transition-wise rubric credit assignment for long-horizon tasks with sparse successful anchors.</p></details> |  |
| **[Certified Execution Harness for Agentic AI: Safe and Least-Intrusive Petri-Net Supervisory Control Between Reasoning and Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31376v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper studies the problem of safe tool execution for large language model (LLM) agents under prompt injection. Using Petri nets, we formalize the execution harness between the LLM and its tools as a supervisory control problem, in which marks represent capabilities, approvals and budgets. Since a transition fires only if the marks it needs are present, an injected request whose preconditions are unmet or already spent is refused regardless of its wording. Treating tool outcomes as uncontrollable, we enforce safety by the conventional maximally permissive supervisor, and this safety transfers to the running system under four assumptions. Moreover, to capture an agent that may propose again after a refusal, we introduce the notion of a replanning closed loop, in which each retry costs an inference call. We show that, in this loop, the conventional supervisor can be arbitrarily more intrusive than a stricter safe one. Furthermore, for a given proposer model, the maximally permissive least-intrusive supervisor is characterized as a value-threshold refinement of the conventional one. Under precise conditions, this refinement orders two concurrent actions by at most one monitor place. On an autonomous release agent, we find by synthesis that safe deployment requires a step that the supervisor can refuse after a rollout starts.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 9 figures, 2 tables. Extended version of a paper submitted for publication</p></details> |
| **[TaxoBench: Can Deep Research Agents Retrieve and Organize? Evaluating the Synthesis Gap with Expert Taxonomies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12369v6)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep Research Agents increasingly automate survey writing, yet existing benchmarks do not jointly test whether they retrieve the papers experts consider essential and organize those papers into paper-grounded taxonomies. We introduce TaxoBench, a benchmark built from 72 highly cited LLM surveys, 3,815 cited papers, and their expert-authored taxonomies. TaxoBench evaluates systems in two settings: Deep Research mode measures end-to-end retrieval and organization from a topic, while Bottom-Up mode provides the expert paper set and isolates organization. We evaluate leaf-level assignments with ARI and V-Measure and hierarchy-level structure with US-TED, US-NTED, and Sem-Path. Across 7 Deep Research Agents and 16 LLM configurations, the best agent retrieves only 20.92% of expert-cited papers, and none of 70 standard Bottom-Up runs reaches the experts' average depth of 4.86. A controlled probe shows that models which match this depth do so by fragmenting the taxonomy, reducing alignment with the expert reference. We further find that raw Sem-Path remains near a no-organization floor even when a newer model generation gains 3.68 pp ARI; after depth matching, humans lead on all 10 matched surveys by 13.27 pp. These results identify retrieval and hierarchical organization as separate bottlenecks and show why hierarchy metrics must be calibrated before they are used to compare models.</p></details> | <details><summary>62 pa...</summary><p>62 pages. This version adds a re-test of three current Deep Research agents (App. L.10) and corrects the Qwen row of Table 2</p></details> |
| **[RIDAR: Safeguarding Research Integrity in Autonomous Research Agents via Automated Detection and Runtime Intervention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31372v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous research agents are increasingly used to automate large portions of scientific research, yet they are prone to research integrity violations (RIVs): behaviors that compromise the validity, reproducibility, or faithfulness of the research process. We present RIDAR, a framework for automated RIV detection and runtime intervention. First, we introduce RIVT, a comprehensive taxonomy of 14 RIV types spanning the full research lifecycle. Building on RIVT, we present RIDAR-post-hoc, a framework to detect RIVs within agent trajectories. RIDAR-post-hoc achieves 85.2% F1 performance on RIV-Gold, a benchmark for RIV detection with 60 expert-annotated trajectories. Applying RIDAR-post-hoc to RIV-Wild, a dataset of 300 agent trajectories across 50 distinct research tasks, we detect 3,964 RIV instances in 99.7% of trajectories, revealing the prevalence of RIVs in current agents. Finally, we present RIDAR-runtime, a framework for dynamically detecting and correcting RIVs during agent runtime. RIDAR-runtime effectively reduces agent RIVs remaining at task completion by 36.8%. We will release our code and data to facilitate future research.</p></details> |  |
| **[AfterDeleteBench: Auditing Post-Deletion Information Persistence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31366v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many LLM agents maintain external persistent memory and derive additional state from past interactions, such as summaries. Deleting an original memory record therefore need not remove information carried by its descendants. We introduce AfterDeleteBench, a controlled behavioral audit for this failure mode in fixed-weight LLM agents. For each synthetic secret, we compare an exposed-then-deleted agent with an otherwise matched agent that was never exposed to the secret, allowing post-deletion recovery to be attributed to the original exposure. In our primary setting, a single query recovers the deleted secret from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in roughly two thirds of cases, while matched never-exposed controls have zero recovery. Crucially, recovery does not require a surviving verbatim copy: when the complete secret string is absent and only separately stored components remain under a public composition rule, exact reconstruction succeeds in 62-99% of cases across Qwen, Mistral, and Phi-3. We also show that the response interface is part of the measurement system: target-bearing replies can be scored as failures when the requested format and parser are misaligned. Finally, we separate reconstruction from write-back and fresh-session recall. Provenance-aware closure eliminates observed recovery in the tested positive-baseline settings, whereas an exact-literal write filter blocks reinsertion but cannot erase information already present. | **[Muffin - A Shared Memory Buffer for Asynchronous AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31355v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rise of Agentic AI systems has changed the landscape of artificial intelligence by enabling strong reasoning capabilities through specialized multi-agent interaction with structured self-reflection. | **[ChatT2: An Adaptive Framework for Developing a Large Language Model-Based Agent for Natural Product Domain Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25620v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific investigations into microbial natural products (NPs) present significant challenges for novices, largely due to the complexity of microbial systems, biochemical diversity, technical skill requirements, and the demands of bioinformatics and data analysis processes. ## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Benchmarking Compositional Generalisation for Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08988v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials play a fundamental role in computational chemistry and materials science, enabling applications from molecular dynamics simulations to drug design and materials discovery. While recent approaches can estimate inter-atomic forces with high precision, it remains unclear to what extent they can generalise to previously unseen molecules. Do they learn the compositional structure of chemistry, capturing how molecular fragments and their combinations determine properties, or do they primarily learn to interpolate patterns that are specific to the training examples? To address this question, we propose a benchmark consisting of four tasks that require some form of compositional generalisation. In each task, models are tested on molecules that were unseen during training, but the training data is chosen such that generalisation to the test examples should be feasible for models that learn the underlying physical principles. Our empirical analysis shows that the considered tasks are highly challenging for state-of-the-art models, with errors on out-of-distribution examples often an order of magnitude higher than on in-distribution examples, even when using foundation models that have been pre-trained on millions of molecules.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations & Datasets Track). 63 pages, 27 figures. Code: https://github.com/nourollah/goal ; Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/AmirMasoud/GMD</p></details> |
| **[Distillation, Self-Training, and the Fragility of Learned Chemical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24199v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automating synthesis planning for small-molecule drug design requires chemical reasoners that are accurate as well as open to inspection and adaptation, yet frontier language models remain unreliable at predicting reaction outcomes and difficult to deploy, while small open-weight models lack the necessary domain reasoning. We show that frontier supervision can serve as a one-time bootstrap for open models on forward reaction prediction. Our models, Resonance, distill an open-weight teacher's reasoning traces and then learn from their own verified outputs, turning a small base model with near-zero accuracy into a capable reaction predictor, while a stronger student surpasses its teacher through distillation alone. Accuracy, however, does not bring robust reasoning: the small student inherits its teacher's single-pass style, self-training mainly sharpens answers it could already reach, and it readily abandons correct answers under pushback, suggesting that chemical reasoning models should be evaluated on the robustness of their reasoning as well as on the accuracy of their answers.</p></details> | 10 pages, 7 figures |

Although structure-based drug design (SBDD) has advanced rapidly with deep learning, most generative models still optimize binding to a single target and disregard selectivity due to limited training data and unknown off-target interactions. Motivated by this gap, we introduce SELECTBENCH, a comprehensive benchmark assessing the selectivity of generative SBDD models. SELECTBENCH provides three curated evaluation tasks reflecting different real-world drug discovery scenarios: literature-based case studies, safety screening panels used in pharmaceutical development, and large-scale structure-based evaluation sets. We further introduce a suite of metrics to quantify selectivity and conduct a systematic evaluation of nine representative SBDD models, including methods explicitly trained for selectivity and widely-used inference-time guidance techniques. Our results show that even selectivity-trained models achieve only modest off-target discrimination and inference-time guidance provides limited gains, underscoring the need for method improvement. SELECTBENCH provides both a characterization of where current generative SBDD stands on selectivity and an open, extensible platform to accelerate progress. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/lovetaro/selectbench.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a Presentation at the Neural Information Processing Systems 2026</p></details> |

Here we introduce DrugHarness, an evolvable agent harness coupling composable scientific execution with conditional skill induction. Its environment integrates over 80 scientific operations, biomedical resources and task-specific code, preserving associations among candidates, results and artifacts. From execution trajectories and outcome feedback, DrugHarness induces action hypotheses with observable applicability conditions, recommended actions and supporting evidence. An independent review agent evaluates candidate skills derived from these hypotheses through source verification and, when needed, targeted execution trials. Admitted skills guide subsequent tasks and are revised through feedback. Evaluation on MolBench and SMDD-Bench demonstrates capabilities in molecular screening, optimization and constrained design. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent loop in which an affinity agent, a druggability agent, and a co-optimization agent jointly produce edits. Careful tuning of the autoencoder is crucial to obtain stable, reliable results. Once this tuning is performed and defining training optimality as when the training is stable and the FID score is low and stable as well, the optimal capacity (or number of trainable parameters) of the classical discriminator scales exponentially with respect to the capacity of the quantum generator, and the same is true for the capacity of the classical generator. This hints toward a type of quantum advantage for quantum generative modeling.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, 7 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Chemical QA: Evaluating LLM's Chemical Reasoning with Modular Chemical Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21318v3)** | 2026-01-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While large language models (LLMs) with Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning excel in mathematics and coding, their potential for systematic reasoning in chemistry, a domain demanding rigorous structural analysis for real-world tasks like drug design and reaction engineering, remains untapped. Current benchmarks focus on simple knowledge retrieval, neglecting step-by-step reasoning required for complex tasks such as molecular optimization and reaction prediction. To address this, we introduce ChemCoTBench, a reasoning framework that bridges molecular structure understanding with arithmetic-inspired operations, including addition, deletion, and substitution, to formalize chemical problem-solving into transparent, step-by-step workflows. By treating molecular transformations as modular "chemical operations", the framework enables slow-thinking reasoning, mirroring the logic of mathematical proofs while grounding solutions in real-world chemical constraints. We evaluate models on two high-impact tasks: Molecular Property Optimization and Chemical Reaction Prediction. These tasks mirror real-world challenges while providing structured evaluability. By providing annotated datasets, a reasoning taxonomy, and baseline evaluations, ChemCoTBench bridges the gap between abstract reasoning methods and practical chemical discovery, establishing a foundation for advancing LLMs as tools for AI-driven scientific innovation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2025 Dataset Track, 22 pages, 10 figures</p></details> |
| **[ReACT-Drug: Reaction-Template Guided Reinforcement Learning for de novo Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20958v1)** | 2025-12-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo drug design is a crucial component of modern drug development, yet navigating the vast chemical space to find synthetically accessible, high-affinity candidates remains a significant challenge. Reinforcement Learning (RL) enhances this process by enabling multi-objective optimization and exploration of novel chemical space - capabilities that traditional supervised learning methods lack. In this work, we introduce \textbf{ReACT-Drug}, a fully integrated, target-agnostic molecular design framework based on Reinforcement Learning. Unlike models requiring target-specific fine-tuning, ReACT-Drug utilizes a generalist approach by leveraging ESM-2 protein embeddings to identify similar proteins for a given target from a knowledge base such as Protein Data Base (PDB). Thereafter, the known drug ligands corresponding to such proteins are decomposed to initialize a fragment-based search space, biasing the agent towards biologically relevant subspaces. For each such fragment, the pipeline employs a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) agent guiding a ChemBERTa-encoded molecule through a dynamic action space of chemically valid, reaction-template-based transformations. This results in the generation of \textit{de novo} drug candidates with competitive binding affinities and high synthetic accessibility, while ensuring 100\% chemical validity and novelty as per MOSES benchmarking. This architecture highlights the potential of integrating structural biology, deep representation learning, and chemical synthesis rules to automate and accelerate rational drug design. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/YadunandanRaman/ReACT-Drug/.</p></details> |  |

## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Proprioceptive Safe Active Navigation and Exploration for Planetary Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08905v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deformable granular terrains introduce significant locomotion and immobilization risks in planetary exploration and are difficult to detect via remote sensing (e.g., vision). Legged robots can sense terrain properties through leg-terrain interactions during locomotion, offering a direct means to assess traversability in deformable environments. How to systematically exploit this interaction-derived information for navigation planning, however, remains underexplored. We address this gap by presenting PSANE, a Proprioceptive Safe Active Navigation and Exploration framework that leverages leg-terrain interaction measurements for safe navigation and exploration in unknown deformable environments. PSANE learns a traversability model via Gaussian Process regression to estimate and certify safe regions and identify exploration frontiers online, and integrates these estimates with a reactive controller for real-time navigation. Frontier selection is formulated as a multi-objective optimization that balances safe-set expansion probability and goal-directed cost, with subgoals selected via scalarization over the Pareto-optimal frontier set. PSANE safely explores unknown granular terrain and reaches specified goals using only proprioceptively estimated traversability, while achieving performance improvements over baseline methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to IEEE/RSJ IROS 2026. 8 pages, 7 figures</p></details> |
| **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. We address both gaps with a large opensource dataset and a suite of standardized benchmarks. The dataset contains more than 100 operational amplifier (opamp) topologies (1-, 2-, and 3-stage) in the SkyWater130 PDK, each shipped with structural and functional annotations, topology-specific sizing constraints (symmetry and sizing rules), an Ngspice testbench, and a multi-objective NSGA-II sizing run producing around 103 Pareto-optimal and 106 intermediate sizing-performance pairs per topology. On top of this data we define six benchmark tasks with precise protocols: inverse sizing, topology selection, sub-circuit classification, structural circuit generation, Pareto-membership prediction, and multi-objective optimization. Thus, the data presented here constitutes the most comprehensive dataset for ML development in over four decades of analog IC design automation. The dataset, ground-truth annotations, and reference implementations of the metric-defining routines are released under an open license, enabling reproducible and directly comparable evaluation across heterogeneous analog design automation methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>Datas...</summary><p>Dataset available on zenodo: https://zenodo.org/records/21759759 9 pages, 6 figures, Conference: MLCAD '26: 8th ACM/IEEE International Symposium on Machine Learning for CAD, September 7 to 9, 2026, Jeju, South Korea</p></details> |
| **[BIRD-RL: Scaling Agentic Reinforcement Learning over Stateful Data-Centric Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29426v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While agentic reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for training small language models (SLMs), existing frameworks are predominantly tool-centric and struggle with data-centric environments. Specifically, applying tool-centric frameworks to stateful tasks like SQL debugging introduces severe spatial inefficiencies since proper environment isolation typically requires a separate database container per rollout. Naively reusing databases without tracking evolving states across multi-turn interactions causes trajectory contamination and cross-trajectory interference, which severely destabilizes policy updates. To overcome these bottlenecks, we introduce BIRD-RL, a novel four-stage agentic RL curriculum that alternately enhances foundational agent reasoning and trajectory-adaptive on-policy exploration to circumvent the instability inherently associated with direct end-to-end learning. Crucially, to support this curriculum, BIRD-RL is driven by a Trajectory-scoped Persistent Execution Infrastructure, which enables safe database replica reuse while preserving trajectory isolation. This design improves spatial RL efficiency by 12.8x, successfully enabling scalable agentic RL for complex data-centric tasks without needs of Kubernettes. Empirical evaluations on the BIRD-CRITIC-SQLite benchmark demonstrate that our 7B and 14B models achieve Success Rates of 44.40% and 48.00%, respectively, showing competitive performance against strong proprietary model-based agents such as Claude-Opus-4.6 Agent (48.20%). Furthermore, BIRD-RL successfully trains unified SLMs capable of both advanced SQL debugging and generation, highlighting its broader potential for multi-objective optimization across heterogeneous, real-world data-centric tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 Main Track</p></details> |
| **[SliMOO: Interpretable Multi-Objective Evolutionary Search for LLM Depth Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27812v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) grow more powerful, their scale makes them costly to serve. However, we observe that transformer layers exhibit markedly different task-dependent layer contributions, whereas existing depth-pruning methods ignore this heterogeneity and thus tend to optimize for a narrow domain or benchmark rather than preserve broad capability. This suggests that LLM depth pruning should be formulated as a multi-objective optimization problem rather than a single-score ranking problem. We propose SliMOO, an interpretable multi-objective evolutionary search framework for LLM depth pruning. SliMOO first uses single-layer removal probing to construct a task-layer importance map, which provides an interpretable view of task-shared and task-specific layers and serves as a search prior. It then performs a Pareto-aware evolutionary search for pruning masks, where candidates are generated under the guidance of the task-layer prior, scored by their deviation from the dense model on each task, and retained through NSGA-II environmental selection. Across the Llama-3.1 and Qwen3 model families, SliMOO consistently achieves stronger multi-task trade-offs than rule-based, greedy, and scalarized baselines. For example, on Llama-3.1-70B at 25% sparsity, it improves the math-domain average from 24.33% to 34.40% and the code-domain average from 35.03% to 39.14%. Code will be made available at https://github.com/AMD-AGI/SliMOO.</p></details> |  |
| **[Jacobian Descent for Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.16232v4)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many optimization problems require balancing multiple conflicting objectives. To generalize gradient descent, which is limited to single-objective optimization, we introduce Jacobian descent (JD) and its stochastic variants. This algorithm iteratively updates parameters using the Jacobian matrix of a vector-valued objective function, in which each row is the gradient of an individual objective. While several methods to combine gradients already exist in the literature, they generally struggle when the objectives conflict. In contrast, we propose projecting gradients to fully resolve conflict. We prove convergence to the Pareto front in the smooth convex case with this approach, a stronger guarantee than the usual convergence to a weakly Pareto stationary point. Our method also enables instance-wise risk minimization (IWRM), a novel learning paradigm in which the loss of each training example is considered a separate objective. Early experimentation on image classification shows promising results for IWRM. Lastly, we provide an efficient implementation of JD using the Gramian of the Jacobian matrix to drastically reduce memory requirements.</p></details> | <details><summary>53 pa...</summary><p>53 pages, 14 figures, Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster)</p></details> |
| **[Communication-Efficient Federated Domain Generalization in Edge Networks via Dynamic Prototype Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25810v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated learning (FL) is a distributed paradigm for Internet of Things (IoT) edge networks. However, deploying Vision-Language Models (VLMs) over wireless edge networks presents distinct challenges: large model parameters increase uplink transmission latency over bandwidth-constrained fading channels, and non-independent and identically distributed (Non-IID) data exacerbates client drift. Existing feature alignment solutions often introduce auxiliary computations that consume time slots allocated for wireless parameter transmission, increasing the likelihood of straggler dropouts under system delay constraints. To address these issues, we propose DPGR-GCAM, a communication-efficient federated domain generalization framework. By employing a dual-parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) architecture, we design a channel-aware tiered transmission mechanism that adaptively truncates payloads to maximize node survival rates under fading conditions. To mitigate client drift, we introduce the Gradient-Conflict Aware Modulation (GCAM) mechanism. GCAM operates within the low-dimensional feature manifold to dynamically resolve multi-objective optimization conflicts with minimal edge computation latency, thereby reducing computation-induced network outages. Experiments demonstrate that DPGR-GCAM improves cross-domain generalization and sustains convergence under network fading scenarios with a 10\% client participation rate.</p></details> |  |
| **[Python Tool for Visualizing Variability of Pareto Fronts over Multiple Runs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.08852v2)** | 2026-10-17 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hyperparameter optimization is crucial to achieving high performance in deep learning. On top of the performance, other criteria such as inference time or memory requirement often need to be optimized due to some practical reasons. This motivates research on multi-objective optimization (MOO). However, Pareto fronts of MOO methods are often shown without considering the variability caused by random seeds and this makes the performance stability evaluation difficult. Although there is a concept named \emph{empirical attainment surface} to enable the visualization with uncertainty over multiple runs, there is no major Python package for empirical attainment surface. We, therefore, develop a Python package for this purpose and describe the usage. The package is available at https://github.com/nabenabe0928/empirical-attainment-func. OptunaHub now provides our implementation at https://hub.optuna.org/visualization/plot_empirical_attainment_surface/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to AutoML workshop</p></details> |
| **[On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.</p></details> |  |
| **[ParetoChat: An LLM-Based Interface for Interactive Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23062v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization models are often deployed in settings where decision makers (DMs) must balance some conflicting criteria. In practice, however, stakeholders must translate their qualitative preferences between different criteria into numerical weights, priorities, or reference points required by optimization methods, which can be difficult to specify and interpret. This paper proposes \emph{ParetoChat}, an LLM (large language model)-based expert-agent interface for interactive multi-objective optimization. The agent translates natural-language user input into structured scalarization parameters, invokes an optimization system, presents candidate solutions, and iteratively elicits feedback. We describe a multi-agent architecture that separates input processing, multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) expertise, solution presentation, and feedback processing. We then present a simulation study using an LLM-based DM agent and benchmark ParetoChat against interactive methods implemented in DESDEO, including reference-point and NIMBUS methods. An application to historical courier-order assignment instances illustrates how users can explore objective extremes and intermediate trade-offs through natural-language requests. The results indicate that LLM agents may provide a practical interaction layer between business stakeholders and optimization tools.</p></details> |  |
| **[Progressive Lower Bound Evaluation for Urban Road Renovation Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15554v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban infrastructure degrades over time, necessitating periodic renovation to maintain functionality and safety. When renovation is delayed beyond the infrastructure's remaining lifespan, costly emergency interventions become necessary to prevent failure. Decision makers must therefore balance expected emergency intervention costs against traffic congestion impacts. We formalize this trade-off as a road network maintenance scheduling problem with uncertain deadlines, which presents optimization challenges including computationally expensive evaluation and an exponentially growing solution space. We formulate the problem as a bi-level multi-objective optimization problem that explicitly accounts for uncertain infrastructure lifespans through probabilistic failure models. We develop a progressive lower bound evaluation method: candidate schedules receive quick, optimistic estimates of their traffic impact from machine learning based surrogate models, and these estimates are refined by exact traffic simulation only for promising candidates, so that full evaluation is reserved almost exclusively for schedules that can advance the Pareto front. Embedded in a multi-objective genetic algorithm, this evaluation strategy improves solution quality by enabling many more iterations within a fixed computational budget. We demonstrate the method's effectiveness and scalability on substantially larger problem instances than previously addressed in the literature: 76 and 80 projects on the Sioux Falls and Anaheim networks, respectively. Under an identical 24-hour computational budget across 30 random seeds, the method completes up to 87 times as many optimization iterations as standard NSGA-II and achieves statistically significant improvements across multiple performance metrics over both standard NSGA-II and a surrogate-assisted baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 12 figures. Accepted manuscript of the article published in Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cacaie.2026.100174</p></details> |
| **[In-Context Preference-Conditioned Pareto Set Learning from Limited Offline Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21736v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization involves diverse preferences over conflicting objectives. Rather than returning a finite set of trade-off solutions, preference-conditioned Pareto set learning seeks to map a specified preference directly to a corresponding Pareto optimal solution. Yet in many real-world scenarios, optimization must proceed offline without further objective evaluations, leaving only a limited static dataset that sparsely covers the decision and objective spaces. Existing methods either optimize separately for each preference or train a problem-specific Pareto solution model, requiring repeated search across preferences or repeated training across problems. We introduce in-context preference-conditioned Pareto set learning (ICPSL), a framework that amortizes both preference-wise solution search and problem-specific model construction. ICPSL pretrains a foundation-style hypernetwork on a large and diverse set of synthetic prior data. The generated model can then be reused to predict solutions for arbitrary preferences without problem-specific training or preference-wise search. Experiments on synthetic and real-world problems show that ICPSL produces high-quality Pareto solutions while providing substantial speedups in problem-specific solution-model construction.</p></details> |  |
| **[First Mathematical Runtime Analyses of Evolutionary Multitask Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21311v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To facilitate the solving of complex problems, evolutionary multitask optimization is regarded as a promising technique due to its capacity for cross-task knowledge transfer. | **[When the implementation matters more than the algorithm: NSGA-II and other multi-objective optimizers in engineering design tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22397v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Engineering design tools increasingly embed optimizers as ready-made components that users select by algorithm name, while operator settings and bound, integer and duplicate handling stay fixed inside each tool. Four NSGA-II implementations differed by up to 5% in hypervolume, more than three canonical algorithms of different families differed from one another, and their response to population size differed by up to 10 percentage points; for two MOEA/D implementations, by 28. On ZDT test functions the same NSGA-II implementations differed up to 470-fold in IGD. An ablation in one open-source NSGA-II reproduced the behaviour of one tool from its documented settings but not the gaps of the other two, so documented parameters do not identify an implementation. Counting the budget in wall-clock time reversed the ranking of the model-based optimizer, and reporting the final population instead of the archive understated some configurations by up to a third; most differences fell below the surrogate's error. For optimizers embedded in design-support systems, the implementation is part of the method; a reporting checklist is derived: implementation and version, effective settings, budget in time and evaluations, and archive-based indicators.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables. Per-run data, emulator models and analysis scripts: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23157761</p></details> |
| **[Enabling Validation for Robust Few-Shot Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04713v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge in Few-Shot Recognition (FSR) is the scarcity of labeled task-specific data for training. Contemporary methods mitigate this challenge by exploiting pretrained Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for their learned transferable features. Some recent ones augment the scarce data by retrieving relevant yet out-of-distribution (OOD) examples from external data sources. However, they largely overlook an equally fundamental challenge: the absence of validation data for model selection and hyperparameter tuning. As a result, the adapted VLMs overfit the few-shot training data and generalize poorly to OOD test data. We show that retrieved data provide a natural source of validation, but their direct use creates a fundamental paradox. Since the retrieved samples are inherently OOD w.r.t the few-shot ID training data, adapting a VLM to the ID data constantly degrades its performance on the retrieved data. Consequently, conventional validation relying on the accuracy of retrieved data favors no adaptation and prevents improved generalization. To resolve this dilemma, we formulate validation as a multi-objective optimization (MOO) problem between performance gains on the few-shot ID data and the performance preservation on the retrieved data. We introduce a simple yet effective validation strategy by choosing an operating point on the Pareto front of the MOO problem. This strategy enables hyperparameter tuning, which had been unrealistically done by using test data, thereby mitigating overfitting and improving generalization. We integrate it into a stage-wise finetuning pipeline, termed Validation-Enabled Stage-wise Tuning (VEST). Extensive experiments on five established benchmarks show that VEST consistently outperforms existing approaches on both ID and OOD test data.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project website: https://hannawang09.github.io/projects/vest/</p></details> |
| **[Large Language Models for Model-Based Robot Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33423v3)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) can contribute useful engineering knowledge to robot design, but directly generated designs may rely on implicit assumptions and provide no guarantees of feasibility or optimality. The model can then be updated with additional engineering, manufacturer, or system-specific information before formal multi-objective optimization provides feasibility and Pareto-optimality guarantees with respect to the finalized model and specified design space. We evaluate the framework on quadcopter and line-following robot component-selection problems. Across 30 direct LLM design trials, none could be verified as feasible under the corresponding finalized model. Comparisons with an independently developed expert model and successive stages of model refinement further showed that changes in modeling assumptions substantially altered the predicted feasible and Pareto-optimal design sets. Together, these results show that using LLMs to construct explicit engineering models makes the underlying design choices available for inspection and revision before those assumptions determine the optimized designs. Explicit modeling therefore provides an interface for combining LLM-generated engineering knowledge, system-specific information, and formal design optimization.</p></details> | 9 pages, 4 figures |
| **[When Rewards Disagree: Reward Weighting for Token-Efficient Video Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.04415v4)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Human perception interprets video dynamics by constructing structured mental representations of entities, actions, and temporal boundaries before engaging in high-level reasoning. However, post-training a model to extract structured evidence while reasoning introduces a severe multi-objective optimization bottleneck, as candidate rollouts must simultaneously satisfy schema compliance, task accuracy, and reasoning budget. Across temporal grounding and video question-answering benchmarks, GRAB generates 13% to 39% fewer tokens while maintaining competitive accuracy with exhaustively hand-tuned static weights -- without requiring hyperparameter weight sweeps.</p></details> |  |

Across SecRepoBench and BaxBench, Harmony Harness consistently outperforms strong harness-evolution baselines, with gains exceeding 10% on both benchmarks. Our results show that explicitly modeling both objective trade-offs and harness structure yields substantially stronger and more transferable agent evolution than existing single-objective, monolithic approaches.</p></details> |  |
| **[SURF: Steering the Scalarization Weight to Uniformly Traverse the Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20619v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scalarization is widely used in multi-objective optimization owing to its simplicity and scalability. In many applications, the goal is to generate solutions that represent diverse user preferences, ideally with uniform coverage of the Pareto front (PF). However, uniformly sampling scalarization weights usually induces non-uniform coverage of the PF. We explain this mismatch through the geometry of the scalarization path. As the weight varies, the corresponding solutions traverse the PF at a generally nonuniform speed. Integrating this speed gives cumulative arc length. After normalization by the total length, it defines a cumulative distribution function (CDF) on weights whose inverse selects points at equal arc-length intervals. Building on this insight, we propose SURF (Sampling Uniformly along the Pareto Front). For structured problems, including bi-objective bandits, we derive analytical expressions for this CDF map and the resulting PF-aware weight sampling rule. For general problems, SURF alternates between CDF reconstruction and weight sampling. Theoretically, we show that under provable conditions for bi-objective LS, SURF's CDF reconstruction error converges linearly to an $O(N^{-2})$ discretization floor. Empirically, experiments on bandits, multi-objective-gymnasium, and multi-objective LLM alignment demonstrate that SURF efficiently achieves more uniform PF coverage than baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scheduling in Cloud,Fog, and Edge Computing: A Comparative Analysis and Decision Framework for Technique Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13830v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The increasing heterogeneity and dynamism of cloud, fog, and edge computing make scheduling a challenging problem involving diverse resource capabilities, workload characteristics, latency and energy constraints, and competing optimization objectives. Although numerous scheduling methods have been proposed, existing reviews often provide fragmented coverage of computing environments, scheduling granularities, or technique families, offering limited guidance on selecting an appropriate approach for specific requirements. This review presents a comparative analysis of task, job, and workflow scheduling across the cloud, fog, and edge paradigms, covering heuristic, metaheuristic, learning-based, and hybrid techniques. Studies published between 2020 and 2026 are analyzed according to scheduling granularity, computing environment, optimization objectives, constraints, performance metrics, and evaluation tools. The analysis highlights trade-offs among computational efficiency, optimization capability, adaptability, and implementation complexity across technique families. Heuristic methods offer low computational overhead for relatively stable scheduling conditions, whereas metaheuristic methods are suited to complex and multi-objective optimization problems. Learning-based methods provide greater adaptability to dynamic and uncertain environments but introduce data, training, inference, and convergence requirements. Hybrid methods combine complementary capabilities but generally increase computational and implementation complexity. Based on these findings, a decision framework is developed to guide technique selection according to workload characteristics, environmental conditions, optimization objectives, resource constraints, and computational requirements.</p></details> |  |

## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Data-Driven Soft Labeling Scales DNA Read Classification to Whole-Body Cell-Type Deconvolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04987v5)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cell-type deconvolution, the task of estimating the proportions of constituent cell types in a heterogeneous biological sample, is a core problem in computational biology. Methods that rely on epigenetic marks such as DNA methylation typically operate on aggregated methylation estimates, discarding the pattern-level information carried by individual DNA reads. Existing read-level approaches that exploit this information are scarce, and all remain restricted to few-class settings; scaling them further is an open problem because, at scale, non-discriminative reads dominate and hard labels conflict with the many-to-many mapping between methylation patterns and cell types, preventing classifier convergence. To overcome this, we propose data-driven soft labels that estimate the conditional cell-type distribution for each read, and integrate this scheme into $Syto$, a new modular framework for read-level classification-based deconvolution. On a whole-body atlas of 39 human cell types, $Syto$ reduces MSE by 3.7$\times$ over the best examined baseline, with gains transferring to an out-of-distribution dataset spanning 16 tissues. $Syto$ lays the foundation for modeling increasingly large cell-type panels, with improved applications in biology and healthcare. The proposed soft-labeling scheme is further translatable to any setting with a many-to-many signal-to-label mapping.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track), poster</p></details> |
| **[Secure Multimodal Federated Optimization via Hierarchical DNA-Based Key Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27644v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated optimization over heterogeneous multimodal data enables distributed institutions to collaboratively optimize a shared model using medical images, genomic profiles, clinical records, and physiological signals without centralizing raw data. This paper proposes a secure multimodal federated optimization framework that separates local data protection from federated-update protection. A trusted key-management authority securely provisions one client key through Hybrid Public Key Encryption (HPKE), from which each client locally derives modality-specific symmetric keys using the domain-separated HMAC-based Extract-and-Expand Key Derivation Function (HKDF). Heterogeneous modalities are mapped into a unified DNA-domain representation and processed through a DNA-origami-inspired structural transformation before authenticated encryption with AES-GCM. During local optimization, only the required modalities are decrypted within an authorized client environment. Individual model updates are independently protected through secure aggregation, allowing the coordinator to observe only aggregated updates rather than each client's contribution. Together, these components establish a structured approach to modality-specific protection and secure collaborative optimization, integrating hierarchical key management, authenticated local encryption, and aggregation-level update protection within a unified framework.</p></details> |  |
