| **[Cost-Aware Abstention for LLM-Based IT Ticket Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31657v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wrong automatic routing of an IT support ticket can cost more than human triage. We evaluate cost-aware abstention as an offline, cost-conditional readiness assessment: select a routing policy on calibration data under explicit costs, then evaluate that frozen policy once on held-out test data. On a public 47,837-ticket, eight-class corpus, we compare four trained non-LLM classifiers with four large language models under zero- and few-shot prompting. Under the illustrative cost model (c_ok, c_triage, c_wrong) = (0, 1, 5), TF-IDF with logistic regression and a threshold of 0.60 selected on calibration reduces test cost from 0.763 to 0.472, a 38.1% relative reduction (paired ratio-bootstrap 95% CI [35.7%, 40.4%]), while lowering wrong routes from 15.3% to 3.5%. Frozen calibration-selected policies also beat both always-route and always-defer on held-out test data in 14 of 16 settings in a post-hoc cost-parameter analysis. The strongest prompted configuration reaches 0.477 accuracy on a stratified 1,000-ticket test subset, versus 0.879 for fine-tuned DistilBERT on the same tickets. Isotonic lowers expected calibration error without reliably improving discrimination: GPT-4o-mini few-shot has one confidence value before and after recalibration and correctness AUROC 0.500. We therefore report correctness AUROC and tie-aware risk-coverage area alongside ECE. At the analytically derived break-even probability 0.80, seven prompted configurations defer all tickets; Haiku few-shot routes only 19/1,000. An LLM-only agreement control and margin/entropy rejection baselines further separate calibration from discrimination. The findings are scoped to this dataset, prompt design, model endpoints, and illustrative costs; the work is an evaluation framework, not a new abstention algorithm or universal deployment certification.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 38th IEEE International Conference on Tools with Artificial Intelligence (ICTAI 2026). 9 pages, 5 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |

An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATTUNE: Agentic Throughput Tuning for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17528v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The throughput of an LLM training job depends on its recipe: how the model is split across GPUs, how memory is traded for compute, and which kernels and communication overlaps it uses. The best recipe changes with every model, accelerator and framework version. Automated tuners search a fixed space of knobs and check only that a job runs, so they miss levers outside that space and can ship a configuration that silently changes training. ATTUNE is a coding agent that tunes any working recipe unattended within a GPU-hour budget. Guided by a skill of search rules, scripts and lessons from past tuning campaigns, it diagnoses each run and changes one lever at a time. A correctness gate outside the agent refuses any argument known to change the training objective. ATTUNE tunes Amazon's production post-training recipes on H100, H200 and B200 clusters, raising throughput by 1.7x on average. It beats the published throughput of four of NVIDIA's own benchmark recipes on NVIDIA's AutoModel framework by 6-17%. On models held out from its development, it improves the strongest public recipes most where they were written for another setting, by up to 2.6x on MiniMax-M2, and least where the vendor tuned them for the same hardware. Given the same GPU-hour budget, it outperforms existing automated tuners, including Bayesian optimization, random search and OptiCo, a recent LLM-agent tuner. Its gain on GLM-4.5 is more than four times the best alternative's. The same coding agent without ATTUNE's skill gains less and fails half its launches. | **[Who Is Sycophantic to Whom? | **[Semantics Delivery Network: Rethinking Web Retrieval Infrastructure for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22486v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly ground their answers in proprietary data and live web content through both single-shot retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and multi-turn agentic search. Yet the web path they use was built for human readers: search services return URLs and snippets ranked by generic relevance, and content delivery networks (CDNs) cache URL-addressed objects without knowing which passage an agent needs. LLMs instead consume short, semantically coherent passages - hereafter, chunks - selected for downstream utility rather than similarity alone, retrieve statefully across reasoning turns, and, as uncoordinated streams, repeatedly fetch, clean, chunk, and embed the same content. We argue that semantic chunk retrieval should become a first-class network-delivery abstraction, and propose the Semantics Delivery Network (SemDN): an origin-authorized, hierarchical substrate that indexes, searches, and caches web content at chunk granularity, serving clients and origins alike. SemDN is more than finer-grained caching: it must estimate missing or stale evidence without an explicit miss signal, let clients choose retrieval representations that origins need not see, and share state among clients with the same representation. Preliminary results show that page-first retrieval processes two to three orders of magnitude more content than agents consume, that agentic queries exhibit task-local chunk reuse invisible to URL caches, and that chunk delivery improves answer quality per context token. We organize an open research agenda around shareable retrieval state, hierarchical caching and refresh under coverage risk, and deployability, including incentives, privacy, and verifiability. Our code is available at: https://github.com/sarendis56/SemDN_HotNets26</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted and to appear at the 25th ACM Workshop on Hot Topics in Networks (HotNets 2026). 13 pages, 3 figures</p></details> |

Performance is strongly representation-dependent and no single representation wins across tasks, though CML is the best, followed by MolJSON, InChI and canonical SMILES. Explicit structured text representations (CML and MolJSON) dominate structural tasks; IUPAC dominates semantic tasks, winning molecule retrieval for all 16 LLMs; and SMILES variants are rarely optimal despite their prevalence in pretraining. Chemistry-specialized models perform well with SMILES at the cost of large degradations with structured text representations, suggesting SMILES-only evaluation rewards specialization that does not generalize. Using LLM-as-a- judge, we find that IUPAC produces the highest fraction of correct molecule generations. A mechanistic study via tokenization audits, linear probes, and attention shows that representations are encoded differently inside the model; for example, structured representations require more attention across the molecular span. Our results argue against representation-invariant evaluation and motivate task-aware representation routing for LLM-based chemistry.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 10 figures, 21 tables. Accepted at the Main Conference of EMNLP 2026</p></details> |

Finally, we show that the largest improvement comes from moving beyond English-only supervision: adding a single parallel language yields a substantial gain, while further gains from additional languages are smaller but consistent. | **[Evaluating the Applicability of LLM Inference Optimizations on Apple Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31479v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Apple Silicon is among the most widely used platforms for local LLM inference, yet nearly all inference optimizations are designed and validated on server-class NVIDIA GPUs under batched serving workloads. CUDA kernel designs transfer only partially: public Metal lacks asynchronous copy, and batch-one decode makes several serving-oriented mechanisms inapplicable, although transferable components yield up to 1.20 times decode throughput. Quantization gains vary by machine. Across four families, a reported speedup is not a portable property of a technique alone but a joint function of technique, runtime, hardware contract, and workload regime.</p></details> | <details><summary>Paper...</summary><p>Paper was accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on On-Device Intelligence (ODI) and the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on SLMs for Agentic Systems (SLM-Agents). We propose \textbf{EDRM} (Entropy Dynamics-based Reasoning Manifold), a lightweight routing framework whose default heuristic requires no router training. | **[Major Revision, Minor Accountability: LLMs as Reviewers of Published Articles in Top-Tier Journals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31414v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With the growth of scientific output, the peer review system is approaching a breaking point: reviewers are increasingly hard to secure, and turnaround times lengthen accordingly. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. In multiple-choice probing, language cues shift answers about three times more than explicit cues but away from the associated country's distribution, and explicit cues do not consistently move answers toward the named country. | **[Beyond Prompt and Parser Engineering: Soft-Prompt Tuning for Automated LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12117v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmark scores often misrepresent a large language model's (LLM's) knowledge, because they rely, e.g., on the model's ability to follow specific formatting requirements. | **[SARIA: Persistence-Aware KV Caching with Coordinated DRAM--SSD Reload for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31169v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents usually reuse long context prefixes, making key value (KV) caching crucial for reducing prefill latency. SSDs provide a low-cost extension to limited DRAM capacity but introduce two key challenges. Eagerly persisting all KV cache blocks incurs substantial write traffic and rapidly consumes finite SSD endurance. Meanwhile, the small number of SSDs provisioned in typical GPU servers limits aggregate reload bandwidth, making SSD-resident KV cache much slower to retrieve than that in DRAM and inflating prefill latency. We present \textsc{Saria} (Selective Admission and Reload for Inference Acceleration), a KV caching system that jointly manages DRAM and SSD by exploiting the session-level temporal locality of LLM agents to reduce SSD writes and accelerate cache reload. On the write path, \textsc{Saria} uses eviction and reuse history to selectively persist KV cache blocks that exhibit reuse opportunities beyond their DRAM residency, avoiding unnecessary writes for KV cache already hosted in DRAM. On the read path, \textsc{Saria} promotes selected SSD-hit KV cache blocks from GPU memory into DRAM after prefill, allowing subsequent reuse within the same session to reduce SSD read traffic while limiting DRAM contention. A transfer coordinator paces DRAM reads while SSD transfers are active, allowing both paths to make progress and efficiently share GPU ingress bandwidth. We implement \textsc{Saria} on LMCache MP and evaluate it using workloads from TraceLab and WEKA, covering both full-attention and hybrid-attention models. The results show that \textsc{Saria} reduces SSD writes by 57.1--58.9\% compared with popular full duplicate caching and the mean time to first token (TTFT) by 42.3--79.7\% over DRAM-only caching.</p></details> | 12 pages, 16 figures |

Yet, by preserving correlations between persistent neighbours, they restore consensus in some cases that fail to converge under well-mixed interactions. Networks also govern vulnerability to adversarial attack. We measure the critical mass of committed agents needed to overturn an established convention. Tipping thresholds follow the same ordering across models and conditions: hubs $\leq$ random $\leq$ low-degree nodes. On the most heterogeneous topology, median critical masses are 3%, 16%, and 35%, respectively; targeting hubs requires one-sixth as many committed agents as random placement. | **[ARES: Adaptive Reasoning-Effort Steering for PPA- and Cost-Aware RTL Optimization with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27879v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents optimize the power, performance, and area (PPA) of register-transfer-level (RTL) designs by iterating over edits, synthesis, and PPA analysis, paying a dollar cost for every LLM call. (1) We introduce a normalized dollar cost per LLM call reported alongside the figure of merit (FoM), enabling fair comparison across effort levels and optimizers. The second is Tree-Structured Credit Assignment. We organize sampled trajectories into trees and assign action-level credit using parent--child reward differences and recovery gains. | **[ProCLIP: Progressive Vision-Language Alignment via LLM-based Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18795v4)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The original CLIP text encoder is limited by a maximum input length of 77 tokens, which hampers its ability to effectively process long texts and perform fine-grained semantic understanding. | **[Gandalf: Flash Shall Not Die! Two conventional detectors trained on the laboratory data caught nearly every held-out laboratory fall but raised 14.2 and 23.9 false alarms per day in the home. We release the frame-level fall annotations of the public dataset and every model answer on it.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 6 figures. Data: https://senecta.ai/research/thermal-fall-llm-data.zip</p></details> |
| **[RelAgent: LLM Agents as Data Scientists for Relational Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07840v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Relational learning is a challenging problem that has motivated a wide range of approaches, including graph-based models (e.g., graph neural networks, graph transformers), tabular methods (e.g., tabular foundation models), and sequence-based approaches (e.g., large language models), each with its own advantages and limitations. We propose RelAgent, an LLM-based autonomous data scientist for relational learning, which operates in two phases. In the search phase, an LLM agent uses database, validation, and evaluation workspace tools to construct SQL feature programs and select a predictive model. In the inference phase, the resulting program is executed without further LLM calls. The final predictor consists of SQL queries and a classical model, enabling fast, deterministic, and intrinsically interpretable predictions: features are human-readable queries, and predictions depend only on the resulting query-defined feature map, enabling scalable deployment using standard database systems.</p></details> |  |
| **[HAWK: Heterogeneity-Aware KV Cache Transfer for Efficient GPU-NPU Phase-Disaggregated LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29873v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) inference exhibits distinct computational characteristics across its two phases: prefill is compute-intensive, whereas decode is more memory-bound. As a result, AI accelerators achieve varying levels of efficiency across the two phases. GPUs are well suited to prefill, but are often underutilized during decode. By contrast, NPUs can utilize their compute resources more effectively during decode. Motivated by this observation, we explore a heterogeneous architecture that integrates GPUs and NPUs, mapping prefill to GPUs and decode to NPUs to achieve high performance and efficiency. However, realizing such heterogeneous integration is challenging because LLM phase disaggregation requires KV cache transfer from prefill to decode (that is, from GPUs to NPUs), which is complicated by differences in inter-device communication mechanisms and KV cache tensor layouts. To address this, we propose HAWK, a transfer mechanism that enables rapid KV cache transfer across heterogeneous devices. HAWK establishes a remote direct memory access (RDMA) path for direct cross-device transfers while handling inter-device tensor layout discrepancies as part of the transfer process. In particular, HAWK allocates tensor-layout-aware RDMA buffers on the NPU side to match the target tensor layout. Leveraging these buffers, HAWK orchestrates data transfer from a conventional contiguous GPU RDMA buffer to the corresponding NPU buffers, allowing transferred tensors to be consumed directly without additional data rearrangement while maintaining transfer efficiency. We implement and evaluate HAWK on a system built with commercially available GPUs and NPUs, demonstrating performance within 7.88% of a GPU-only system under the same SLO constraints while achieving up to 1.26$\times$ higher power efficiency and 1.72$\times$ higher cost efficiency.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 13 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[S-EDL: Eliciting Self-Evidence from Sequence Likelihoods for Semantic Calibration of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29832v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Calibrating large language models (LLMs) in open-ended generation is uniquely challenging because many distinct token sequences can express the same underlying meaning. Prior calibration techniques target either fixed-set classification confidence or the binary correctness of individual strings, making them inherently ill-suited for the dynamic, multi-string nature of open-ended semantic outcomes. To bridge this gap, we introduce S-EDL, a novel evidential method that natively optimizes LLMs for semantic calibration. By eliciting evidence from the sequence likelihoods of the model itself, S-EDL constructs a differentiable Dirichlet prior over prompt-specific semantic classes. Optimizing the evidential loss under this prior yields a calibration-aware training signal over variable semantic classes, completely bypassing the need for fixed multiple-choice options. Evaluations across four open-ended benchmarks and three models demonstrate that S-EDL substantially reduces semantic calibration error while improving accuracy. By aligning generative likelihoods with semantic correctness, this work establishes a framework for deploying trustworthy LLMs in complex, open-ended applications. Code is available at: https://github.com/sandylaker/s-edl.</p></details> | 34 pages, 8 figures |
| **[UlaCache: Unified Logical Addressing for Sparse LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29820v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long contexts increase the memory cost of the key-value (KV) cache in large language model (LLM) inference. Sparse attention reduces attention computation and memory traffic by selecting historical tokens, but the retained KV cache must remain available for later decoding steps. We present UlaCache, a KV cache management system built around a unified logical address space spanning GPU KV pools and optional CPU memory. Stable logical references let the inference engine allocate and select KV independently of physical placement. Common allocation and sparse-access interfaces support data-parallel (DP) and tensor-parallel (TP) attention. For distributed storage, local GPU buffers retain selected KV across decoding steps, so only missing entries require transfers. When CPU storage is enabled, Parallel Bucketed LRU (PB-LRU) manages a GPU pool shared across requests. It updates recency metadata and selects eviction victims in parallel. UlaCache transfers KV and then updates the address mappings. With speculative decoding enabled under matched CPU and GPU memory budgets, UlaCache achieves peak decoding throughput speedups of 1.16 $\times$ (DP) and 1.70 $\times$ (TP) on H200, and 1.73 $\times$ (DP) and 2.51 $\times$ (TP) on B300, compared with the state-of-the-art baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[InfoQuant: Shaping Activation Distributions for Low-Bit LLM Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26175v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Low-bit activation quantization remains a major bottleneck in efficient large language model (LLM) deployment. The difficulty is not only that activations contain outliers, but that their distributions are often poorly matched to a low-bit uniform quantizer. Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods suppress peaks, balance channels, or minimize reconstruction error, yet they rarely specify what activation distribution is actually easy to discretize. As a result, activations may appear numerically smoother while still incurring large quantization error because the quantization range remains wide or most values collapse into a few levels near the mean. We recast activation transformation as quantizer-facing distribution design and analyze quantization error from an information-theoretic perspective. Our analysis shows that quantization-friendly activations should jointly have a smaller numerical range and sufficient dispersion within that range. Guided by this analysis, we propose InfoQuant, a train-free method that employs Peak Suppression Orthogonal Transformation (PSOT) to shape activations into more quantization-friendly distributions. We further introduce adaptive outlier-token selection to improve the robustness of PSOT during optimization. Across multiple LLM families, InfoQuant consistently outperforms prior PTQ and end-to-end training baselines. Under W4A4KV4, it preserves 97% of floating-point accuracy on average and reduces the LLaMA-2 13B performance gap by 42% over the previous state of the art. Code is available at [https://github.com/LLIKKE/InfoQuant](https://github.com/LLIKKE/InfoQuant)</p></details> | EMNLP2026 main |
| **[Neither Stochastic Parroting nor AGI: LLMs Solve Tasks through Context-Directed Extrapolation from Training Data Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23323v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This position paper argues that many behaviours framed as either "stochastic parroting" or "advanced reasoning" are better explained as context-directed extrapolation from training-data priors, and that ML and NLP research communities would benefit from evaluations that distinguish remembering and basic extrapolation from genuine application-level generalisation. Our central contribution is an actionable evaluation checklist and design patterns that make these distinctions testable in practice. Our framework posits that LLMs extrapolate from training data priors while using context to guide the model to the appropriate priors; we call this "context-directed extrapolation." Specifically, this context direction is achieved through examples in base models, leading to in-context learning, while instruction tuning allows analogous task specification via prompts rather than explicit examples. We argue that adopting this more realistic framework and controls for improving evaluation would better calibrate claims about LLM "reasoning," refocus research on how LLM performance interacts with training data and generalisability to out-of-domain tasks, and reduce miscalibration in deployment and policy decisions.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 1 figure (including appendix)</p></details> |
| **[Where Do LLMs Learn to Recommend AI?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29785v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Asked what to study, which career to pursue, or what product to build, large language models (LLMs) often recommend AI-related options. We ask where this tendency comes from by comparing models before and after fine-tuning and by varying how questions are asked. First, pretrained models already include explicit AI/ML terms in the recommendations of 27% of their responses, and their released instruction-tuned versions do so in 59%. Second, fine-tuning models ourselves shows that this rise is not inevitable: general instruction tuning leaves the rate unchanged, but removing the 7% of instruction examples that concern AI cuts it by 14 percentage points, below the level of the pretrained models it starts from. Data rewarding helpfulness raise it by 9 points, while data ranked by the preferences of diverse simulated users lower it by 9. Finally, with models held fixed, asking for the "best" option or for what to do "right now" raises the rate by 8 and 18 points, whereas telling the model it is an AI advisor adds 4. Overall, the tendency starts in pretraining, and what models learn afterward, together with how a question is phrased, decides how strongly it appears.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 4 figures. Companion paper to arXiv:2601.13749</p></details> |
